Why had it seemed, for so long, that there was only one way forward, even as he struggled to tear himself away from it?

Miki took long showers with the door locked. He stayed until his skin scalded red and the steam began to eat at the walls. He was finally beginning to form the words.

A month ago there had been nothing for them but down. Nothing but wounding and wound-licking, nothing but descent into codependency and magnification. Kozue’s angry possessiveness swelling within her; Miki’s feigned purity collapsing and rising again in a brilliant, misleading image. There was nothing but their bodies thrown together again, breaking.

He wanted to see her for the first time. For them to actually look at each other honestly. Without the sucking emptiness of romance, or the cruelty of obsession. As family. It would be a long road, and a hard one. But the gates to it were open, and never had been before.

He knew what had happened. In the arena. He didn’t know how, but he knew Juri had also known. The hardest-forgotten memory of all. Just a hint, just a sense of things. There was no way for him to have known it in full, but even the erased recollection of a far-off sound was enough. The impossible, indisputable knowledge that the sword in his heart had there joined a million others, and…

Why could he see so much? Why could he see so much of himself? Why did it hurt so badly? What had Utena done?

Kozue knocked. He turned off the water. He stared at the mirror as if he was going to kill it.

“What are we even doing here? And what the hell are you wearing?” Kyouichi grumbled.

“Patience, patience. I have something to show you.”

Kyouichi shivered in the cold, late fall evening. For some reason he had had trouble finding warm enough clothes, and had only his rather thin denim jacket for protection.

“You have something to show me? Or is this just another errand for the chairman.”

Touga chuckled, and they arrived at the front door of the chairman’s residence.

“The Tower of the Rose.”

Kyouichi snorted derisively, but quickly shut up as Touga made a show of somehow unlocking the door himself.

The elevator ride was utterly silent. Only the sound of the mechanism. It took a strangely long time, with Kyouichi fidgeting the whole way and Touga standing confidently directly in front of the doors, frozen in anticipation.

The counter dinged at last. The doors shuddered open on a place they had never been before, as often as they had once come here; a long stone walkway stretched out before them in red carpet, suspended within raw darkness. There was no other word for it but darkness. The walkway terminated in a far-off platform, where lay a massive engraved purple globe behind a simple coffin.

Kyouichi followed as Touga strode forward.

He spoke with a kind of awe, a kind of rushed wonder. “The chairman is gone. He has entrusted his position to me. You remember, don’t you? You remember how desperately you once longed for eternity? It is here, Kyouichi, It is all here.”

“Gone? Listen, I don’t like this. The gaps. Have we been here before?”

“By one interpretation. But not properly.”

“So what… what is it?

“The power to live. The power to build. The power to revolutionize the world.” They reached the platform, and Touga promptly threw open the coffin with one hand. There, pinned to the wooden backboard by twenty rusting swords and overgrown in white roses, was a pristine white version of the suddenly familiar dress of the Rose Bride.

“And now I need for nothing but a partner.”

Kyouichi started. “I-i-is that why you brought me here? To, to become…?”

He was met by a genuinely baffled look that quickly fell to laughter. “Hahaha, are… what? Are you serious? You really thought that I’d want you to be the Rose Bride?”

The tension broke, and after a moment of confusion the two laughed together. Such absurdity. They laughed without knowing why, like neither of them had laughed since they were kids, for a few long and precious minutes. Giving in, and warding off the pressure.

Touga collected himself first. “No, no, you’re no bride. I want you as my Mikage, as it were. A second person, a complement. I… I won’t have time for study anymore. I want you to come with me, to pick up where his work ended, to truly discover the secrets of this place. I can offer you a tenure, a fellowship, anything really. We can be anything at all, now. I’m not satisfied with acceptance.”

Kyouichi exhaled. He had no idea who Mikage was, but went along with it anyway. “I see, I see. So you’ll become the prince without a bride, huh?”

“No, there’ll be a bride. There has to be.”

“But with Anthy gone… or is she coming back?”

“What? No, I don’t need her. She wouldn’t even work. The chairman had Anthy, and I have Nanami.”

He was speechless.

“Oh, don’t look at me like that. You knew what this was. You were with me at the End of the World. Really, you can’t expect –“

Kyouichi could only step back, trying to process the implications. “What does this mean for her?”

“You saw it. The glittering steel… It means what it has always meant.”

“Are you insane? She’s… she’s… you can’t be serious.”

“Did you still think this game would remain a game? Did you think this was something I was doing for fun? Did you think this place would just be handed to us?”

“I haven’t thought about this place at all until you brought me here. I mean… I mean… you can’t force it on her, she would never accept. She’s not like Anthy!”

Touga met his eyes coldly. “But she already has. You know nothing of what this is. You know nothing of what she and I now are. It seems children like you really can’t understand the value of this place.”

“You’re no chairman.” For once, he didn’t flinch at Touga’s staredown. “You’re better than that, I know you well enough by now. Have you forgotten so soon what he did with us?” He finished in only a whisper.

“What he taught us.”

Kyouichi looked at him like he was insane. “No. This…” he gestured vaguely, “all this sudden grandeur of yours is well and good, but you haven’t fallen that far. I went along with you for all of the duels. I thought that if I did I would see the truth, but… it can’t be this, this…” He trailed off and left a short, pointed silence.

“This what? You’re faltering now that you must look at it more closely? The same thing you’ve done, in less words?”

“What I’m saying is that the whole thing is too terrible.”

“Of course it is terrible.”

“Then why?”

Something in Touga snapped, more fear than anger. “Because the world demands it! The world screams for it! We’re out of time! There must be the supports, or the entire structure collapses!”

“Why… why her, then? Why her?” All he had left that night were his stupid questions.

“Do you only object because you actually care about this one? Blinded by your dreamy, blind chivalry? We don’t have the luxury to believe in love any longer, Saionji!”

Kyouichi winced, on the precipice.

Touga continued, collecting himself slightly. “I’m disappointed. And she is, you know.”

“She is what?”

“She is… like Anthy. They all are.”

He didn’t know how to respond. He didn’t know how to feel.

“If you want to change anything,” Touga said, slowing his breathing, “come to Nemuro. Lead the seminar. For the revolution of the world.”

Some long-strained gear in Kyouichi’s heart groaned over another cog, and he backed away a few steps in a kind of disgust he did not want to understand yet.

“I’m leaving,” he said, and left.

“Coward,” Touga responded, to an empty room.

“Miss Nanami.”

“Hm?”

The rain beat on the glass of the council room. Keiko sat on the top of a stepladder next to one of the archive shelves, poring over records in some binder. Nanami looked out the window and listened to paper rustle.

“I’m just not understanding this. Have you taken a look at these yet?”

“No. They should have been taken care of at the beginning of the year.”

“’Himemiya’? Is that familiar to you?”

Nanami’s chest tightened, but she didn’t show it. “Oh, her? Yes, yes, of course she’s familiar. She transferred. Perfectly explicable.”

“Okay… but… oh, look at this! There’s another one. ‘Arisugawa’! Listed as fencing club captain, and council treasurer! How could we have missed that?”

“I’m council treasurer. And Miki’s been captain since Ruka graduated. Don’t you remember that?”

“Of course I do! Which is why this whole thing is so weird. Why do we have her photo if she never went here? This is, like,” she flipped through her notes, “the tenth or so person I’ve found that I just can’t explain. And that’s just in her year.”

“Probably just an error with the dates.”

“I… I guess, but… ugh! I’ve looked through so many of the past graduating classes, and none corroborate it! All their own fencing captains, everything in order…”

Nanami stood up, looking a bit cross. “Why are you spending so much time on this?”

Keiko was at a loss for words for a moment. “I mean, this is an important project, right? The record reorganization.”

“Yes. But no one asked you to go this far.”

“Still, I… oh, I don’t know. I just feel as if I’m missing something, as if… oh, never mind.”

“Are you feeling well?”

“Yes.”

“Sleeping enough?”

“Y-yes! But…”

“You’re worrying me.” She softened her tone a bit, but kept it authoritative. “Go home, Keiko. It’s late, we’ll call… we’ll call someone down in the morning to help figure this out.”

Keiko sighed, both reluctant and relieved. “Alright, Nanami.”

“…’Nanami’?”

“Oh! Sorry, sorry, I meant –”

“Don’t worry about it. I was just surprised.”

The two were quiet for a moment. The rain let up.

“We should probably get going.”

“You’re right… Nanami.”

They each allowed themselves a very small smile, and did not let the other see it.

Nanami walked home, half-wondering about it all. So much was still fuzzy – the borders between her dreams and reality; all her memories were in disarray. But they were all there. She remembered Juri, of course, it was only that her apparently real memories of her were all tied up in memories of the river, somehow, with the council, with some nonsense about bowling balls… how were you supposed to tell which way was up?

The leaves had all turned brown on the pavement by now, and were slick and dark with the recently passed rain. A lingering, humid warmth cut the cold dusk air, when the purple shadows of sunset just barely blushed at the border of the yawning blue and black. She let her legs walk her home, and allowed her mind to wander just a bit more than usual.

The house was quiet. Dark. She saw the light on in Touga’s room as she approached, but as she came inside saw that none of the others were on. That was strange. The thought “he probably has some girl over” pushed at her and she pushed it away again, violently.

She hung up her umbrella. She loosened her uniform’s tie – the winter uniform, with the awful teal blazer, that hadn’t existed until last month. She took off her shoes. She made her way through the silent and massive house, and opened her door.

The dress stood on its own in the center of her bedroom.

It was white. It was not supposed to be white.

Her brother stepped quietly from the shadows, wearing a new uniform bright against the dark. His hand moved to turn on the light, and her heart fell through the floor.

Detached from the moment, Nanami realized immediately what this was. She also realized that a few months ago it would have worked. She would have melted into his arms even as the terror began to rise in her; she would have succumbed, and let herself harden around the steel. But it was too late. It was far too late. She still remembered it. She remembered Anthy, she even remembered Utena. And she remembered, every waking second, the sound that rushed through the End of the World.

He spoke impossibly gently. “Good. I see you understand, Nanami. Now, knowing everything of this world –”

“Are you trying to sound cool!?” She surprised herself with how loud she shouted. The sense that there should be music playing promptly disappeared and left a close, echoless silence.

He sighed and shifted his stance, unbothered.

“Did you think I would still say yes? After everything?”

“Put the dress on, Nanami.”

“No!”

“Haven’t you seen it?” He stepped forward, straightening his posture. “I know what you’re looking for. I truly do. I want it too, but this is… this is the only way we can be together. You don’t know how special, how precious you truly are… how necessary. We have already come too far. This is what you are.”

“Liar! God, you’re not even good at this!”

“Nanami.”

“I wanted to believe that I’d imagined it, that I was only… Did you change? Were you always like this? Did… did he do something to you? I… I…”

“He only showed me that which was in front of me. Eternity. Our eternity.”

She said nothing.

“Nanami.” At this point he could not help a slightly impatient tone, “Put. The dress on.”

“You don’t get it?” Her voice strained and became faint. “Who… who do you think you are?”

And, in a voice that threatened to swallow the sky, he answered: “The End of the World.”

She turned and sprinted out of the house.

She did not cry. She would not allow herself to cry. She only ran, slipping on the leaves, her breath bursting in little white clouds. She did not cry even when she heard the door slam again behind her, even as she heard Touga following her – begging, cajoling, threatening, cursing. She did not look back, and she did not cry, and his voice immediately fell silent as she clattered over the open thresh-

old of the Academy. She ran, the air cold as a knife at her face, and she did not think about it or cry a drop until she was already at the station ten minutes later, the blind train pulling up in front of a breathless and freezing girl who had nothing in the world but her uniform and schoolbag.

Only then did she deign to cry. She cried hard as the night city flew behind her, and then the moonlit sea. She cried for the weight of his hand on her shoulder and the years of dead selves that accompanied it. She cried for the memory of the name of a cat and the yawning hatred of empty rooms. She cried for Anthy, and she cried worse for Kozue, and she cried terribly for the evil they had all been taught was love. She cried and the world slowly disappeared.

It fell to the distance, and as the melted average of the passing buildings slowly came to a single story among the bare trees, she collected herself. She stopped shaking. She wiped her eyes, and unfolded her limbs which had been drawn like a vise around her bag and shoulders, and finally looked up.

Anthy Himemiya sat directly across from her, wearing a warm pink dress with her hair down. She smiled, and with an unbearable, nonchalant gentleness, she spoke. “It’s good to see you here.”

And in that moment there was nothing else to say, and the world had no roads.

Chapter 6: part four, hatching nanami

And now we know everything of this world. What happens next?

I wrote a short proof-of-concept fanfiction to answer the question “what happens after Anthy leaves Ohtori,” and I guess this essay will be an effort to justify it. I’d recommend reading it before the rest of this post.

Though, in the simplest form I can get it, what happens after is that the doors are open. Utena has made it possible to see outside of the Academy, out of the entire system. That knowledge may be ignored by some people, but even then it will nudge at them subconsciously – Utena has proved, even with no one to witness it, that there is something besides Ohtori’s world. There is a way out.

The thing about Ohtori – the thing about structures in general, really, especially gender – is that for it to continue standing people must buy into it. They must believe the story. Ohtori as a system depends on people upholding the ideals of its own conception of romance, because it works exclusively through people and relationships. There is nothing physical in the entire construct; even the swords are human hearts behind their metaphor.

The only reason our cast tortured itself so much in the series was a shared and deeply held belief in the system. Wakaba aspiring for its specialness, Saionji attempting to make a place for himself, Kozue giving up her purity to make it easier. Everyone sees the whole structure as unshakeable, immutable, default. How could anyone change the world, beyond sanctioned and inaccessible revolution? You either believe in love and try to make it work within the system, or stop believing and try to exploit the system as best you can – but that system in and of itself is never called into question.

Until Utena.

Her giving up, her walking away, is the only way to get out of this trap. She decided that she didn’t actually want to try to make it work anymore. The lie of the prince was exposed and rejected. Two girls who were both called princess and witch walked away without having to be either label. The whole thing was thrown out – maybe only in one relationship, maybe only symbolically – but whatever happened, it happened at the utter core of it.

The duel called revolution remains without a victor. No counter-image has been put forward: the palace is empty. Different people will react to this in different ways. Some will leave the academy, and some will cling even tighter to it. Ohtori will react, too – it is a machine that is more than capable of stabilizing itself when challenged even this deeply. With both Anthy and Utena absent, and Akio panicking at his sudden impotent irrelevance, the entire archetypal trio of figures at the helm of the ship has all but vanished – however, instead of simply collapsing, the edifice will first attempt to reestablish itself upon a different foundational relationship.

Looks like we’ve spent the entire show quietly building up a pair of candidates.

We’re in heavy speculation territory here. I freely acknowledge this is all guesswork, but not the baseless kind. I’m projecting what I think happens next off of my previously laid-out conception of what Ohtori is, what it values, and how it works – namely; child abuse, male power, and an overwhelming system of romantic mythology to keep everyone in line. I’ve seen a truly incredible range of opinions on the next course of events – but based on my own reading, I can only see one way forward: Touga begins to push Nanami into being his princess, at the heart of the world.

The palace is empty. The Kiryuus inherit it. We’ve long since established Touga’s hungry eagerness to take up the position – so the choice in the end is Nanami’s.

And, honestly? We’ve already established what that choice will be.

But we have a few loose ends to tie up before we get into the utter end. We still need to establish a bit more baseline before we reach epilogue territory. There’s still two sibling pairs we haven’t discussed as thoroughly in this series of essays: the Kaorus, and the Arisugawas.

Miki and Kozue — Lightning Strikes the Forest

“Ok, you’ve got me,” a more skeptic hypothetical audience member might be asking me right about now, “power dynamics and knowing coercion is what makes this whole thing so terrible. But what about incest without that? What about the best case scenario? Let me go devil’s advocate here – let’s get rid of the age difference factor, let’s assume the sister is the one initiating in this case – how do you parse that? Can you still condemn it so easily?”

Not as easily, no; there’s the rub. But we can still very clearly condemn it.

I haven’t touched on the Kaorus much, which might come off as weird considering I’ve spent however many words before this yelling about siblings. They comprise our third main pair, one degree more removed from the central archetype when compared to the Kiryuus. This is where the ambiguity kicks in, and makes them harder to parse – they seem more common, more believable almost, than the Kiryuus’ and Himemiyas’ curated conformance and intensity.

Miki and Kozue, for the moment, have the freedom to still be people before the prince and princess story fully claims them. As the youngest and most mutually confused, they deal in more moral greyness, more subtlety, and less explicitness. The Kiryuus can be clearly identified as an emulation of the Himemiyas, but the Kaorus aren’t fully following in the steps of either.

The question here is: we know Touga could be an Akio, but could Miki be a Touga? Could Kozue?

The morals of responsibility get shaky when dealing with this stuff. We can clearly name Akio and Touga as deliberately abusive, but the why there is never just simple hatred or cruelty. That is a part of it, yes, a massive part of it – but they are looking for something in doing what they do to their sisters. Akio is looking for an eternity that has been rotting for a long time; Touga is looking for power in a world he feels powerless in. Both, in their abusiveness, dig themselves deeper into what they flee, and perpetuate the structure.

What are Miki and Kozue looking for?

Kozue is looking for purity. For childhood. For a return to her previous innocence, which was taken from her too early. Somewhere between neglectful parents and a stream of hungry boys there was a darkening of her world, a dirtiness that she does not see in Miki. Maybe if she capitalized on that dirtiness, she can push herself hard enough to get back. Where else has she ever been happy besides with Miki? Why doesn’t it feel right anymore? Those questions don’t matter. She just needs to make it work one more time.

Kozue, in Miki’s last duel, accepts her own version of the rose bride’s dress, standing next to Miki perfect in his princely council uniform. She has brought him here, she has determined that this relationship is the only route to finding what she’s looking for. “Hey,” she says to Anthy, sidling up next to her, “Tell me how to be just like you.”

I don’t think Kozue knows what being like Anthy would mean for her. She thinks she knows - but she has desensitized herself only to the more shallow modes of power-weighted sexuality, and does not understand Anthy’s state. This speaks volumes on what she thinks she and Miki are – she does not truly want to move towards Akio and Anthy’s relationship, only backwards into her and Miki’s old one.

Miki is looking for belonging, and to make sense of himself. The two were raised in an environment of fancy neglect, and for a long time only had each other. Miki has opened up a bit more, become more involved in student life, but has distances himself from his idea of home. Kozue does the same thing, but more loudly while still clinging to it, but Miki does it in truth. The fact that he even has a sister blindsides us the first time we learn about it.

What is he running from? Maybe she has grown past him for the first time, and he doesn’t want to admit it. Maybe he is scared of her, in some way. Maybe he wants to break from his past, convinced it has no place for him, seeing it as a ruined place instead of Kozue’s vision of innocence and peace. Maybe he remembers more, and does not want to.

So he looks elsewhere, and stumbles into Anthy and Touga, of all possible people. I’m sure those two have healthy advice for him.

The Kaorus’ songs talk about time. About returning to the past; about embodying the past, present, and future. Will we find eternity if we go far back enough? If we regress enough? If we return to an idea of childhood only to find that it has already been tainted and changed by a false romance?

Miki and Kozue have lost something. A once-familiar dynamic that did not require words has somewhere along the way fallen out of sync, and left both of them never having learned how to talk about it. They are in an era of reformation – trying to figure out how they feel about each other.

We see here how Ohtori perverts formerly normal relationships. Each of them looks to the world for an answer on what to do next, and the world answers “obsession and possessiveness”. Of course everything will go back to comfortable normalcy if Kozue can manage to get closer to Miki again, and while spending time with Akio and all the other boys she learns what getting closer again would require. Of course Miki can find a place he belongs in again, as long as he can manage to find a special love again – one that, Touga teaches him, must have been romantic.

They have lost each other, and look for each other in the wrong places, with the wrong motivations.

Are the Kaorus outright abusive towards each other, currently? Maybe not. I don’t think so. This comes off as just general miserable unhealthiness to me, not entirely instigated by either of them, where each of them seem to be hurt by it in the same way. Do they have the potential to be abusive, however? Absolutely.

The world is pushing them in the direction of romance. There is a path prepared for them. They each take on a kind of cruelty. Miki borders on chauvinism in his attitude towards Anthy, and Kozue has gotten very attached to her jealous “impurity”, but it’s hard for me to name either of them as fully responsible for this weird codependent mess. At Ohtori, when one comes to know the way the world works, they do one of three things: reject it, pretend it does not exist, or accept and embrace it: Miki is trying very hard to ignore it, and Kozue is attempting to embrace it.

The key here is that neither of them are actively seeking to hurt the other. Neither of them are, properly, looking for power. Kozue might want to shock Miki into action, and Miki may want to just stop being confused, but their possessiveness comes in the form of ideas towards a goal that is something other than possessiveness. It has been the idea of romance being the only kind of love they can have that makes them so unwilling to look at each other. They are each worried that if they do, they will see something they don’t actually want.

But Kozue has convinced herself she wants it. It is easier that way, after all. And Miki flinches first, looks back, and sees it. They have one duel together, one moment of tense, stilted prince-princess playing-house – and then they break apart again.

The last we see of them, Kozue has crawled back to the piano, but keeps her distance. I’d like to believe they are slowly finding a way through.

Juri and ? — Crossing the River

I really didn’t like writing this one, but I can’t ignore it.

Juri… where do we begin with Juri? I guess we start at where she starts at, so often: Shiori.

For a long time I didn’t understand why Juri loved Shiori. I couldn’t see what she saw in her. It seemed irrational from the outside, as if Juri was just being naïve and willfully blind about what Shiori is like. Even with nearly every character having similarly miserable romantic obsessions, something about how she relates to Shiori just didn’t click for me. It is more subtle than our usual fare.

Juri has never been able to move through the world in the way Shiori has. Shiori in her eyes is almost ethereal, walking without touching the ground; a sort of mysterious ambiguity which looks almost like detachment from the grand social game. Shiori is dreamy, impulsive, vague in all her mannerisms. I’d call it childish, in both the innocent and cruel sense: Shiori tears the wings off butterflies. Is this just weird sadism, or a curious and scientific mind?

Maybe this is an engineered mystery. Shiori moves as smoothly as Touga does through the rote of social positioning, and if she understands that she has this charm to her, she certainly has also learned to weaponize it. We see her utterly joyous when she gets to hurt Juri – does she do this just for the pleasure of it, or is she just a confused, resentful, limited girl only knowing half of the story?

Regardless – Juri insists on believing the best of her. I think she is almost fascinated with Shiori’s freedom, her ease and lack of immobilizing worry in her life. I get very strong images of someone shielding a kitten from a fire even as it claws at their breast. Juri is only holding out for the day when the rebellious phase ends; the day that it works, the day that she knows her feelings – and hopes to learn something in the process. Until that day, it doesn’t really matter what Shiori does.

I’ve found that the key to understanding these two is the fact that Juri’s love for Shiori is a truly unconditional one. It is almost as if she is trying to prove something to herself.

Ikuhara in one of the commentary tracks spoke about why the romanticization of incest works. In his words, it is a relationship that is permanent by nature. It is the idea of two people sharing the same body, or one person living in two bodies. A shared self, a home. Intimacy. Unconditionality. This is a screaming and blatant lie, though sometimes a comforting one, and everything that takes this as a base will be founded on its lie.

The concept of the eternal castle finds its origin here.

Please bear with me:

I think it’s interesting that in a world so defined by attempts at heterosexual sibling relationships, our most explicitly lesbian character also breaks the pattern by having an older sister instead of brother. I think it’s interesting that this is revealed at the utter emotional climax of the series, in a cutaway at the same time Utena has just been backstabbed to the ground.

I think it’s interesting that the single reference to Juri’s sister is involved with the river leitmotif. We see the river with Nanami’s cat, with Adolescence’s Touga, and with Juri’s sister. Something about sacrifice for love. Something about irreparable early loss. Something about the death of children seeping into one’s identity.

Juri has so clearly seen through so much of the world, but still finds herself bound to it. Why is this? What was it that made her see? Is it only her history with Shiori; is she somehow this disillusioned while still so fervently believing?

It’s very easy for me to read Juri as a survivor of sibling abuse, of some kind. Her situation is unique in that it has already been a long time since, unlike Nanami, and in that she has actually gotten away, unlike Anthy. It’s very easy for me to see all her stilted, angry coldness as an attempt to cope with that. Her distance, her resentment of everyone else’s frivolity a reaction to being exposed earlier to more blatant modes of how the world works.

It’s very easy for me to parse her fixation on Shiori an attempt to rediscover or rebuild an eternity she may have been promised and denied – to build a new story to define herself by as a way to outgrow an older and more terrible one. Juri’s, Shiori’s, and even Ruka’s songs uniformly focus on the concept of duality, of being two opposite things at once, speaking to a deep confusion and sense of wrongness that I believe Juri is trying to find a way out of.

This is an extremely bold and extremely serious assertion, with less basis and less sense even than anything I’ve said about Kanae. I realize that fully, and honestly apologize for it. I definitely don’t want to posit this as canon, only as one option of how to read things – there’s an argument to be made here. I don’t bring this up just for the hell of it.

If we have established anything in these essays, it is that being a little sister in Utena means something, and in Juri’s case, it worries me. Over the course of the show’s 39 episodes, it rains exactly twice: once for Nanami, and once for Juri.

Let’s leave it at that.

Eternity

Why does Utena focus on sibling abuse so intensely?

This isn’t a story that’s told very often. It is very rarely talked about so frankly, much less sympathetically. A big part of what makes Utena so important to me is that it dares to tackle the issue with all the gravity and integrity it requires. This show is genuinely the only fictional work I’ve seen that is both mostly about discussing the subject, and dedicated to condemning it.

Utena does take incest as a kind of archetype and metaphor for abuse as a whole, but in doing so doesn’t shy away from discussing it in specific, in the context of trends and mechanisms specific to it. The savior prince stems directly from the protective older brother, and the waiting princess directly from the petulant little sister – but this is a story anyone can tell themselves. Romance as an idea has been perverted. Blood poured into the water at the source of the fountain. Pain becomes universal, and cheap.

Fate; an enduring and validating narrative to justify your life’s course. Specialness, the shining thing; the idea of, even if just once, hearing “it has to be you, it can’t be anyone but you”. Eternity; security, stability, an end to fear and anxiety. True love. Belonging. Home.

This is what we look for in intimacy. This is what all human hearts yearn for. At Ohtori, and in our own world as well, we have been lied to about where to find this. The narrative of incestuous romance capitalizes viciously on the desire for all these things. It promises an easy route to all of them; a place that inherently has them. It lies through its teeth, for one purpose only.

We asked this question at the beginning of Part Two and were only able to answer it through different characters’ limited views. But now we can answer it for real:

What is incest?

It is, in its ideal form, eternity. It is something that never ends. It is something you can never get out of you, or escape from. Eternity is a lack of change, a lack of growth; a preserved, static, stagnant portrait. It is the idea that one never has to grow or mature again. The promise that everything is just fine as it is, that there is a place that will accept you as you are, that you do not have to worry about. This promise is empty.

Those who build systems like this fear change and maturity because it undermines their own power. It makes it harder to lie and manipulate. They are happy with eternity because they do not want to relinquish their power to some idea of “human respect” or whatever. They want to remain as they are, forever: positioned to take advantage of anyone in their spell for the sake of shallow powertrip pleasure of convenience, at the cost of ruined lives.

The promise of “you are perfect as you are” turns very quickly into “you are perfect when you are exactly as I want you, and worthless otherwise”. It can’t be lived up to naturally, and requires a violently enforced warping of the very self. If Anthy has grown accustomed to where she is, it is out of last-resort coping and adaptation, not out of an aboriginal love of being stabbed.

Eternity and revolution are opposite concepts.

It is, by its nature, abusive. It is a relationship that cannot work and does not even believe itself to be able to; only pretending to for the benefit of its victims. In some ways it is the first instance of abuse – the closest and earliest it can happen to a person; a midpoint between parental abuse and non-familial peer abuse that encapsulates the worst of both.

The pressures of family make romance impossible. There is always an element of power in any familial relationship, and this dynamic runs even deeper than non-familial ones. Someone is older, someone is more easily believed, someone is physically stronger. There is the blanket cultural expectation of loyalty to family that makes even saying no so much more difficult, not to mention speaking out about it. Incest always happens in a kind of violent secrecy, that must obscure the harm it does in order to continue. It is a capitalization on a pre-existing, natural kind of love; ruining what should be one of the most lasting and supportive relationships a person has in their lives out of an incomprehensibly strong selfishness.

This doesn’t just show up naturally. This ever happening depends on a betrayal of a very fundamental human trust. It is the erosion of the ability to believe in anything; to believe in a family that won’t hurt you out of shallow self-obsessed entitlement, to believe in an idea of love that is based on anything other than violence, sex, and power.

It is the legitimization of abuse. I mentioned Utena having an attitude of universal condemnation, and that is very much an exception. Works that focus on incest tend to either treat it as an unnuanced shock-value plot device, or else build it up into an idealized romance between equals whose darkest bits only come from outside taboo attitudes. From Game of Thrones to Oreimo, we either see it consistently brushed to the side or viciously sold as innocuous forbidden love. Even Saitou’s After the Revolution chapters are guilty of this – continuing to write Nanami out entirely, and presenting Miki and Kozue as healing from the events of the show through embracing the very relationship that caused the injury in the first place. If this is all we ever see of it in fiction, it either seems outlandish or unimportant in real life, and feeds into the constant refrain of those who do not want to understand: “What’s so bad about it, after all?” “Weren’t you just kids experimenting?” “Haven’t you moved on yet?” and finally, “You let it happen.”

It is the lie of consent; that all “yes”s are equal. It is an obfuscation of social and personal pressures that can change one’s will; this insistence that being groomed since birth can somehow produce feelings that are still naturally your own. No victim comes into this willingly. None of us actually fall in love. Some were never under any illusions about this, and some most certainly are.

Anthy “loves” Akio because she has been robbed of everything else in her life that she could hold on to through the swords’ pain. Nanami “loves” Touga as the results of a years-long campaign to produce that in her, to make her dependent and obsessive via a constant environment of manufactured unreality. Kozue “loves” Miki because she has been convinced that retreating into unhealthy, childish specialness is the only way to stay afloat in a world that does a lot worse to girls than longing. These are false loves, based on fear, on desperation, on intense guilt and blame, on a Stockholm loss of options.

All they are looking for is a place to anchor, and the only option given is one that hates them.

This image replicates itself. If it can be done here, it can be done anywhere.

Eternity never lasts, in any of the names it sells itself by. It cannot. Eternity isn’t real. We start to realize. Juri has probably known for a long time, but is still in denial. Anthy and Utena abandoned the concept entirely. And now, Nanami…

Nanami comes to turn away from it also. She turns away because she has understood two things: one, that she is not alone, and two, that there is a way out. The lessons that she has learned at the end are the same ones we must take with us if we hope to make any dent in our own culture – one that is disturbingly similar to Ohtori.

Conclusion

Nanami starts the show off as a joke. A caricature, a classical trope. We’ve seen Nanamis a hundred times before, especially in anime and manga. The entitled brat. The spiteful rival. The popular bully. She has a polished ojou laugh, sneers with every motion, even has a gaggle of lackeys. She is as much archetype as she is shoujo stock character. In another series she could be a true antagonist.

For a fourth of the show she is sold as a side character, and much of her screentime consists of gags even beyond that. Nanami is thrown around by elephants, has cutesy misunderstanding escapades, all absurd and over the top. Surely this is a girl who exaggerates. Surely she is just prone to the drama and weirdness. Surely her cruelty is as senseless as it is pathetic. Surely we should look at her as an enemy to our real heroines. A witch to be defeated.

And then we see more of her.

When we see Touga place the rose crest onto Nanami’s finger, we realize that we’ve already been told this story. It is the same story as the one Utena tells to herself at the beginning of each episode, trying to hammer it even further into her skull.

When we finally see Anthy, young and in the farmhouse, worrying herself sick over her unresponsive and distant brother, we realize that we’ve already been told this story. It is the same story as Nanami with her hair down, helplessly peeking into Touga’s depression room with the phonograph blaring.

Nanami is on the exact same path as Utena and Anthy. Princesshood.

Nanami’s character for much of the series hinges on confusion. She is constantly pushing herself to keep up with this set of shifting goalposts, to sort out all her competing feelings. She is a knot of a girl. She has this desperate need to simultaneously grow up and retreat into her past, which translates naturally into chasing after Touga to achieve both. Touga understands everything, and if she can just make him like her again, she’ll understand too. We know this.

But how does that illusion break?

It happens in one place. It begins collapsing when Touga plays with her heart just a bit too far, deteriorates past salvageability when she learns about Anthy, and is entirely out of her system at the end of her last duel. She will need time to put things together, but her suspension of disbelief has already been pushed too far. She has seen something so clear and so terrible that she can no longer believe in it.

Ohtori depends on belief. Nanami has fallen out of love, and no longer is as blind as is required for the grooming protocols to work on her. Touga jumped the gun on her, went too fast, was a bit too honest – and now, she knows. Maybe not everything about how the world works. Maybe not all the theory and dynamics around it. But she certainly knows the truth of what incest is, and the allegiances towards it of those most in power.

Through the lessons of Anthy and Utena, Nanami most of all has learned that there is more than this. There is no metaphysical fate around her neck, there is no loyalty she owes to the world. What those two dared to accomplish echoes down to her; exposing the structure that so desperately depends on silence and invisibility. It is hard to see, especially so suddenly. Nanami reacts in the short term with an emphatic retreat into normalcy, into chaban, but it can’t hold. She can only go up, or out: up to the false promise of specialness, or out into a better world.

Even before the end of the show Nanami has made her choice. It is a tentative one – a nervous one, which still clings a bit to denial – but her little racquetball speech tells us enough. “There was nothing, nothing at all,” and maybe that “nothing” is kinder than the “everything” of the palace. She has seen it all for what it is: the rigged dueling game, the castle that existed in image alone, the song and dance of cajoling predation. All pathetic immaturity in the end. A culture of idiot adolescence.

Utena is about breaking from the narratives of abuse. It is about breaking from the miserable modes of existing that the world insists are the only ones available to you, and building something else. It is about coming into the fullness of your self – on your own terms, and independently from all those who would control and hurt you. Utena is about building a space where this is possible.

This can only be done when we know everything of the world, and turn away from it nonetheless.

Nanami makes it out because she sees the violence that she has faced is not only her own. She does not carry this pain alone. In Anthy, in Utena, in Kozue, even in Juri and Miki she has seen kindred spirits, and come to understand that she is not all that special, after all. The world grinds down on everyone. When she begins to understand the true and full extent of the damage it has done both to her friends and enemies, she understands that it is bigger than her, and can no longer accept it.

This is not a story that is often told. We do not often tell stories about sisters in love with their brothers. We do not often tell stories about brothers destroying their sisters. We do not often tell stories about girls groomed since childhood by tall dark strangers, nor do we often tell stories about women trapped in empty lives where even the spark of the lie has faded. We do not often tell stories about the boys and men who have experienced the same thing.

We do not often tell stories about girls finding strength in each other. We do not often tell stories about women striking back at not only the people that have hurt them, but the very concepts that have enabled those people’s evil in the first place. We do not often tell stories, in this world, that are strong enough to end it.

But Utena does. And Utena does.

We can’t be fooled. There is more to the world than this empty, self-serving dynamic. This is not some natural, unavoidable rule of existence. There is more! More to life than this cycle of ownership and victimhood, more to love than violence and desperation. We do not have to be what the worst parts of this world ask us to.

We have to expose the structure for what it is, in all the places it has claimed. And we can provide that light, no matter our supposed and enforced powerlessness.

We have to talk about it. We have to tell our stories. We have to take stories like this seriously. We have to speak it, in all the ten thousand years of poetry it requires, because for people to break themselves out of it they must know what Nanami now knows. You are not alone. None of us have to be alone in this. That is as comforting as it is terrible, and there is work to be done to be worthy of it.

We have roads to build. We have princes to kill. If the chick does not break its shell, it will exist as a living corpse until it quietly disappears.

And Nanami is hatching.

— Fin —

Kudos: 179 · Bookmarks: 101 · Hits: 3,624

Ensayo crítico · 1973

Bleak House
Alex Zwerdling

Alex Zwerdling

An accurate assessment of Dickens’s portrait of Esther must, I think, begin with his interest in first-person narratives. In David Copperfield, Bleak House, and Great Expectations he used this technical device to record in depth a long process of psychological development. Dickens’s (and the reader’s) attitude toward these narrators is seldom straightforward. Our perspective must be detached and critical, since we are intended to see more than the narrator sees. In all three novels, Dickens further makes use of a double perspective even in the narrator by shifting back and forth from his childish to his adult vision. This temporal double perspective is much more consistently used in David Copperfield and Great Expectations than in Bleak House, however, because in that novel Dickens is interested in portraying someone who remains trapped between childhood and real maturity. Such a narrator is not a transparent medium for the author’s impressions. We are asked to look very much at Esther rather than through her, to observe her actions, her fantasies, even her verbal mannerisms with great attention. For Dickens’s attitude toward her is essentially clinical, and the major aim of her portion of the narrative is to study in detail the short- and long-range effect of a certain kind of adult violence on the mind of a child.

“The crime against the child,” as Dorothy Van Ghent has suggested, is one of Dickens’s major themes. Between Oliver Twist and Bleak House, his vision of childhood suffering became much more psychological. Oliver’s deprivation is primarily physical, external. By the time Dickens came to write Dombey and Son, he had become more interested in the child deprived of love than of food and shelter, and this shift is clearly reflected both in David Copperfield and in Bleak House. Esther’s first memories of her aunt (the “godmother” who brought her up) obliquely record both the withholding of love and the overwhelming sense of failure it imprints on the child: (…)

Esther is wounded by her godmother’s speech but she is not crushed. She has a supremely practical turn of mind, and her first impulse is to formulate a strategy for survival. “Submission, selfdenial, diligent work” is what her godmother proposes. Esther’s own plan is a subtle but significant variant: “I would try, as hard as ever I could, to repair the fault I had been born with (of which I confessedly felt guilty and yet innocent), and would strive as I grew up to be industrious, contented, and kind-hearted, and to do some good to some one, and win some love to myself if I could” (chap. 3). An instinct for self-preservation is at work in this formula. Although she has been told to feel only guilt, she also feels a paradoxical innocence. And the grim words “submission, self-denial, diligent work” are transformed into more social and less self-destructive terms: contentment, kindheartedness, industry. Above all, Esther sees even those devices as means to an end: to “win some love to myself if I could.” It is as if the child instinctively understands this basic psychological necessity and determines to direct all its energies toward obtaining it.

Esther envisages love as a reward for strenuous effort, however, not as something that may come naturally; and she does not specify what kind of love she is talking about. It would, of course, be absurd to expect a child to distinguish among the various kinds of love, parental, romantic, conjugal, filial, and so on. But in fact the ambiguity of the phrase illuminates Esther’s most important adult conflicts. As we gradually begin to understand, she has great doubts about her right to love and marry, and she accepts with misgiving the role of an affectionate spectator of other people’s attachments. There is a buried but potent feeling that the “sin” of being illegitimate makes her unfit for romantic love.

In this impatient command, Esther temporarily resigns her own hopes of romance and begins to live vicariously in the love of Richard and Ada. She cannot treat the idea of having a lover as a serious possibility or as her right. She begins to think of herself as (and allows people to treat her as) “a methodical, old-maidish sort of foolish little person” (chap. 8). She offers no objection to being called a whole string of pet names—“Old Woman, and Little Old Woman, and Cobweb, and Mrs. Shipton, and Mother Hubbard, and Dame Durden, and so many names of that sort, that my own name soon became quite lost among them” (chap. 8). These names have a double function: they substitute the image of an old, maternal, drudging woman for the image of a young girl; and they suggest that the only fit mate for such a person is an aging, paternal man like Mr. Jarndyce. [muy sutil esto último]

Her buried romantic impulse is almost entirely invested in her vicarious participation in the Ada-Richard courtship. She is the Nurse in the lovers’ acting out of Romeo and Juliet, and her fantasies are obviously filled with displaced romance: “They brought a chair on either side of me, and put me between them, and really seemed to have fallen in love with me, instead of one another” (chap. 13). When the marriage finally takes place, there is very little romance left. Richard is nearly penniless and driven half-wild by the Chancery suit; Ada marries him in secret against her guardian’s wishes. When Esther realizes that their marriage will exclude her, as all marriages must, she is driven wild with grief: “I walked up and down in a dim corner, sobbing and crying” (chap. 51). On the same evening, she steals back to their house and stands by their door, listening for “the murmur of their young voices” (chap. 51). After the courtship, the duenna must turn voyeur.

The strangest aspect of Esther’s relationship to the lovers is her attitude toward Ada. Her intense attachment is irrational and mysterious and no commonsense explanation of it will serve. In part, Esther’s adoration of Ada is a displaced form of romantic love, inhibited from expressing itself normally by her fear that she is forbidden to marry. But she has also from the first treated Ada as an idealized second self, as the girl she might have become if she had not been born “different from other children” and “set apart.” Her fantasy-identification with Ada makes it possible for Esther to bear a great deal of humiliation and deprivation. It is as though she lived in the belief that no evil could affect her alter ego, no matter what happened to herself. When she realizes she is ill, she commands her maid to bar the door to Ada: “Charley, if you let her in but once, only to look upon me for one moment as I lie here, I shall die” (chap. 31). Not “she will die,” but “I shall die.” Her need to protect Ada is an oblique form of self-preservation. When, after her disfigurement, she calls Ada “my beauty,” as she frequently does, the phrase is charged with meaning. [esto demuestra que el resto del libro es el despliegue del plan de Esther, un plan de preservación, más sutil e inteligente de lo que podemos captar todo el tiempo porque Esther es inteligentísima, y que se relaciona con la represión, proyección, y el trauma. Me hace recordar un poco a las estrategias defensivas ante el trauma tan avanzadas que muchas veces ha mostrado Julio.]

If Dickens were describing Mrs. Badger in his own voice, only the last phrase would be out of place. It would have to be made obviously (rather than ambiguously) ironic or else eliminated altogether. But for Esther to draw in her claws and play the kitten in this way is essential to her image of herself. Her critical sense is often disguised as mere reportorial accuracy. When its satiric content becomes too dangerous, it is suddenly replaced by a fraudulent generosity of spirit. It is no wonder that critics are confused about Esther’s intelligence and tempted by the theory that she becomes, at moments, merely Dickens’s mouthpiece. But the difference between Dickens and Esther as narrators lies not in their perceptiveness but in their self-confidence about their perceptiveness. The savage sarcasm of the other narrator of Bleak House is simply a psychological impossibility for Esther.

It should be clear from these illustrations that Dickens’s interest in Esther is fundamentally clinical: to observe and describe a certain kind of psychic debility. The psychological subject matter of Dickens’s later novels demanded a new narrative technique, in which the character could present himself directly, rather than being described and interpreted by an omniscient narrator. Esther Summerson is Dickens’s most ambitious attempt to allow a character who does not fully understand herself to tell her own story. There are obvious dangers in such a technique. The reader may not be able to see beyond the narrator’s vision, although he needs to do so if he is to comprehend the book. Furthermore, a neurotic character will be obsessive, and the narrative will inevitably keep recording his obsessions, often at the price of variety. Certainly Bleak House does not escape these dangers. Yet the portrait would be less subtle, detailed, and vivid if it were not done in the first person. Esther’s story must be told from the inside; and if we are to feel that the damage to her psyche has in some sense been permanent, it cannot be told by a fully mature woman looking back on the troubles of her youth.

Esther is also the representative of another inarticulate character in Bleak House—Lady Dedlock, whose inner history Dickens could not write for a Victorian audience. The two women seem totally different yet are in a similar position. Each is alienated from her true self and unable to acknowledge her deepest feelings. Each is incomplete: Lady Dedlock searches for a child; Esther for a mother. Lady Dedlock has given up her lover and married an affectionate and protecting man a generation older than herself, whom she respects but does not love—the very doom that Esther narrowly escapes. The mother’s haughtiness and isolation are as much aspects of a frustrating role as the daughter’s cheerfulness and immersion in the community. The two women specialize in opposing parts of themselves rather than allowing their complex natures full play.

II

I have suggested that Esther is not a static character, and that Dickens is interested in tracing various patterns of behavior in her from infancy to adulthood. In becoming the ward of Mr. Jarndyce, Esther is given a chance to relive her childhood with a radically different parental figure, generous, loving, and open. Consequently she acquires two entirely different images of herself, the rejected and the sponsored child, and these two identities engage in a long civil war for control of her psyche. It is the task of her early adult years to absorb the new image and permit it to affect every aspect of her life, her sense of her own abilities and rights, her assessment of other people, and her life plans. Dickens has a remarkable feeling for the difficulty of this task and understands how potent the curse of childhood deprivation can be, no matter what may happen in later life.

The blossoming of Esther’s working intelligence is a continuous and uninterrupted process in the book. The same can hardly be said for her realization that she has a right to love and marry a man to whom she is attracted. Her romantic nature is much more completely crushed than her intelligence, in this case with the complicity of her guardian and her closest friends. It is surprising that her need for a lover has survived at all, in no matter how buried a form; but that it is very much alive despite all discouragement is evident for the first time when Mr. Guppy proposes. Esther’s reaction to the offer of marriage from this absurd and vulgar man is totally unexpected yet, I think, completely convincing. She is anything but grateful, and her decisive rejection of him introduces a tone which we have never heard before in her words, and will seldom hear again: “Get up from that ridiculous position immediately, sir, or you will oblige me to break my implied promise and ring the bell!” (chap. 9).

The haughty dismissiveness and sheer command of these words sound more like Lady Dedlock than Esther Summerson. Esther here finds herself face-to-face with the kind of man who might be willing to take a piece of damaged goods, and her spirit rebels [tan pero tan sutil esto, y he visto a hombres intentando hacer esto tantas veces para salir con el rabo entre las piernas como aquí] violently against the insult to her own pride. Her response suggests an extremely healthy sense of her own innocence and worth, a facet of her nature that is so regularly repressed that it can only declare its existence in such unexpected explosions. Esther’s later reaction to the scene illustrates better than anything else in the novel how clearly Dickens connects adult with childhood experience:

But, when I went upstairs to my own room, I surprised myself by beginning to laugh about it, and then surprised myself still more by beginning to cry about it. In short, I was in a flutter for a little while; and felt as if an old chord had been more coarsely touched than it ever had been since the days of the dear old doll, long buried in the garden.

The doll had been the sole companion of her childhood, the faithful listener to whom she could tell her troubles without fear of rejection or reproach. When her godmother dies, she buries the doll in the garden as if in penance. The ceremony reveals her guilt about any form of self-indulgence, even such a sorry substitute for maternal acceptance. Its return in her fantasy at this moment shows how Dickens uses childhood associations to explain adult behavior. For the doll is a symbol of her “selfishness,” her need for someone who loves her absolutely. Faced with the prospect of the parody-lover Mr. Guppy, the doll springs inevitably into her fantasy. It will not stay buried, just as her adult truce with the past cannot altogether prevent the occasional “coarse” reassertion of the primary feelings of childhood.

Yet the pride and self-assertion evident in the scene with Guppy desert her completely when she encounters a man to whom she is fully attracted, Allan Woodcourt. It is at first impossible for her even to acknowledge her interest. Her references to Woodcourt are incredibly tortured and coy; yet she is being neither “insincere” nor girlishly silly. She is so terrified of losing him that she can hardly bear to mention his existence. Her first reference to Woodcourt is absurdly stilted and confused, especially for such a lucid and methodical narrator:

I have omitted to mention in its place, that there was some one else at the family dinner party. It was not a lady. It was a gentleman. It was a gentleman of a dark complexion—a young surgeon. He was rather reserved, but I thought him very sensible and agreeable. At least, Ada asked me if I did not, and I said yes.

This might be the “before” exhibit in a grammar book exercise on how to construct complex sentences. Esther regularly becomes a grammatical cripple whenever she mentions Woodcourt: “I believe— at least I know—that he was not rich.” “I think—I mean he told us— that he had been in practice three or four years.” “And so we gave him our hands, one after another—at least they did—and I did” (chap. 17).

Her grammatical disarray is an expression of her anxiety. Deeply convinced that her illegitimacy makes her unfit to marry a man of worth, yet desperately hoping that the conviction is false, she turns Woodcourt into a test case. To acknowledge her feeling for him is to invite disappointment; hence the evasiveness and insincerity of practically everything she says about him until he actually proposes. Since Woodcourt loves Esther, and since there are no real obstacles to their marriage, one might have expected their romance to proceed uninterrupted and Esther’s self-confidence to grow as steadily as did her trust in her own intelligence. But the plot presents two crucial yet seemingly arbitrary setbacks: her illness and consequent disfigurement, and her guardian’s proposal of marriage.

The effect of both these events is regressive. The loss of her beauty convinces her that any thought of marriage is now out of the question. The ambiguous phrase in her childhood vow, to “win some love to myself if I could,” must now be interpreted to mean only the communal affection of those around her, not the romantic love for which she longs. After seeing her new face in the mirror, she tries desperately to convince herself to be satisfied with what she has. This attempt clearly takes her back to an earlier stage, to her childhood vow: (…)

But to rededicate oneself to an ideal formulated in childhood, particularly if it has already become a reality, is to make further progress impossible. Esther is in fact ready to go beyond the vow at the moment of her illness; but her disfigurement makes it impossible for her to proceed. She seems trapped in the present and resigned to surrendering the future.

The treatment of Esther’s illness is difficult to explain on the literal level: she loses and then regains her sight without explanation; her disfigured face is magically restored to its former beauty; Dickens even conspicuously avoids giving the illness a name. Yet as a symbol of Esther’s psychological state at a particular moment in her development, it makes a good deal of sense. It comes when the circumstances of her birth—the fact that she is Lady Dedlock’s child—are exposed. At about this point in the novel, her mother recognizes and reveals herself to the child she had long thought dead, and Mr. Tulkinghorn acquires the crucial piece of evidence that will make the facts public. The symbolic connection of these events with Esther’s disfigurement seems clear. Her illegitimate birth is no longer a secret. She is made ugly in the eyes of the world. Her scarred face is the outward and visible sign of an inward and spiritual sin. Her two sharpest anxieties, about her birth and her disfigurement, come together in chapter 36 in which Lady Dedlock reveals herself and Ada sees her for the first time since her illness. Esther’s violent reaction to her mother’s revelation, which brings her to the nadir of her own self-confidence, feeds directly into her fear that Ada will not recognize her new face.

Her despair is suicidal. She is “possessed by a belief that it was right, and had been intended, that I should die in my birth; and that it was wrong, and not intended, that I should be then alive.” And she reflects on “the new and terrible meaning of the old words, now moaning in my ears like a surge upon the shore, ‘Your mother, Esther, was your disgrace, and you are hers. The time will come— and soon enough—when you will understand this better, and will feel it too, as no one save a woman can’ ” (chap. 36). It is as though the childhood curse had until this moment slept in her ear, for Esther has consistently evaded a fuller knowledge of the circumstances of her birth. But at this point in her life, when she stands at the threshold of maturity, the bare facts must be brought into the open and faced.

She recovers from the terrible despair into which her mother’s revelation has thrown her, however, and the passage in which she declares her own innocence has often been quoted to suggest that her sense of confidence is fully restored. This seems to me a misinterpretation, though not an obvious one. Esther says: “I knew I was as innocent of my birth as a queen of hers; and that before my Heavenly Father I should not be punished for birth, nor a queen rewarded for it” (chap. 36). Although this has the ring of confidence, it should be noticed that Esther is planning to be vindicated in death, not in life. God will recognize her innocence and heaven will bring the rewards she has been denied on earth. But this reliance on a heavenly reward barely masks her despair about the possibility of earthly fulfillment. She has indeed already resigned herself to giving up Woodcourt until heaven where, she says, “I might aspire to meet him, unselfishly, innocently, better far than he had thought me when I found some favour in his eyes, at the journey’s end” (chap. 35, italics added).

In such passages Esther tries to construct a possible life out of the elements of duty, selflessness, and the hope of a heavenly reward [Esto me parece lo más agudo de este análisis: ese egoísmo o sentido de supervivencia que instrumentaliza la magnanimidad para poder sobrevivir como psique]. She struggles to convince herself that they will do; but they will not. There is a strong intuitive awareness that these elements can only be oppressive if they are not combined with self-realization. These buried misgivings are brilliantly suggested during Esther’s illness, as her fantasy takes over from her conscious mind. In her feverish reverie, she identifies her present household tasks with all the former duties of her life. The stream of reminiscence inevitably leads back to her godmother’s house and oppresses her with the sense that every stage of her life, including the supposedly happy present, has merely presented her with a new set of “cares and difficulties.” At this point she begins to hallucinate:

I am almost afraid to hint at that time in my disorder—it seemed one long night, but I believe there were both nights and days in it—when I laboured up colossal staircases, ever striving to reach the top, and ever turned, as I have seen a worm in a garden path, by some obstruction, and labouring again. . . . I would find myself complaining “O more of these never-ending stairs, Charley,—more and more— piled up to the sky, I think!” and labouring on again.

Dare I hint at that worse time when, strung together somewhere in great black space, there was a flaming necklace, or ring, or starry circle of some kind, of which I was one of the beads! And when my only prayer was to be taken off from the rest, and when it was such inexplicable agony and misery to be a part of the dreadful thing? (chap. 35)

In these dreams, Esther can no longer force herself to be content. The frustration of a life made up solely of duties is sharply expressed. Her obligations “mount up” like the endless staircases of her dream; there is no surmounting them in this life. The image of the starry circle probably expresses her intense though unacknowledged need to break out of the chain of commitments to the community, “to be taken off from the rest” and be a separate self. The whole passage is a powerful example of Dickens’s use of fantasy, association, and dream to reveal elements in the psychological makeup of his characters that are systematically repressed in action and in speech.

Esther’s illness (and the shame of her illegitimacy which is so closely related to it) has made her greatly more dependent on Mr. Jarndyce. As her obligations to her guardian increase, her sense of gratitude begins to mix with a kind of bleak helplessness. His protection envelops her; thanking him becomes a full-time duty, even a burden. Just before she reads Mr. Jarndyce’s proposal of marriage, Esther exclaims, “I thanked him with my whole heart. What could I ever do but thank him!” (chap. 44). The question seems rhetorical but its answer is critical for her development. To spend her life in thanking Jarndyce is to surrender to the obstacle in her path.

Her guardian’s proposal, coming at this point, is like Esther’s illness, no arbitrary twist of the plot but a symbol of a particular moment in her development. The marriage would freeze her at the stage of filial dependence; she would be forever safe and adored. It is precisely because her relationship to Jarndyce has been a substitute for so much—a mother’s love, a father’s guidance, the sense of being accepted despite her birth—that it threatens to become her final human tie. But such sponsorship is a stage, hopefully leading to the child’s sense of his own independent power and right of choice. If it lasts forever there has been a crucial failure in the educational process.

This, it seems to me, is the meaning of the proposal. The setback of her illness and the full revelation of her illegitimacy make Esther retreat to the safe world in which she knows she is loved. Yet when she realizes that the door may shut upon her there forever, she is profoundly disturbed. Her first reaction to Jarndyce’s offer of marriage is far from joyous. She thinks of the proposal

as the close of the benignant history I had been pursuing, and I felt that I had but one thing to do. To devote my life to his happiness was to thank him poorly, and what had I wished for the other night but some new means of thanking him?

Still I cried very much... as if something for which there was no name or distinct idea were indefinitely lost to me. I was very happy, very thankful, very hopeful; but I cried very much.

Esther sees her marriage to Jarndyce as an end, not a beginning, and she is convinced that she has no choice. (“I felt I had but one thing to do.”) Although she does not understand exactly what she’s giving up in accepting him, it becomes clear enough a few paragraphs later. Even after her illness she had kept some flowers that Woodcourt had given her, although she doubted then whether she had a right to keep them. Now they must be destroyed. She puts them for a moment to the lips of the sleeping Ada, recalls Ada’s love for Richard, and then burns them. The ceremony is the first act of a novitiate.

Esther’s deeply unsettled feelings about her engagement are also evident in her unwillingness to make it public. She delays as long as possible in telling anyone about it and then regularly insists on using the euphemism that she will become the mistress of Bleak House. The feeling that she is marrying the house rather than its owner is entirely appropriate. At the same time, Esther’s busy cheerfulness begins to show signs of hysteria: “I resolved to be doubly diligent and gay. So I went about the house, humming all the tunes I knew; and I sat working and working in a desperate manner, and I talked and talked, morning, noon, and night” (chap. 1). Violent industry is Esther’s characteristic response to misery she cannot acknowledge.

Yet even immersion in work will not allow her to ignore her fundamental need to be loved passionately rather than paternally. Her romantic longing has fastened on Woodcourt, and at this moment in her history he must therefore reappear. It is worth noting how many apparently arbitrary plot elements in Dickens are determined by his need for symbolic manifestations of internal states. When Esther sees Woodcourt again, she at first instinctively avoids his glance because she is “unwilling that he should see my altered looks.” She has a strong impulse simply to run away, but she forces herself not to do so: “No, my dear, no. No, no, no!” (chap. 45). Her resistance is the first indication since her illness that her willpower can be used to confront her self-doubt rather than suppress it. Only very gradually does Woodcourt’s love convince her that she is not hopelessly tainted, “disfigured,” after all. When she is ready to entertain this radical idea, she is finally ready for his offer of marriage.

The proposal intensifies rather than resolves Esther’s problems, however, because it confronts her with a need to choose when she is not yet psychologically ready to do so. She is still bound to her guardian. Her first response to the knowledge that she can be loved passionately, that there are in fact no obstacles to normal life for her, is not joy but violent regret: “O, too late to know it now, too late, too late. That was the first ungrateful thought I had. Too late” (chap. 61). Her natural impulse is rebellious. She feels that her engagement, her dependence on Jarndyce, fetters her, but the bond is too powerful for her to break.

She is terrified not of hurting Jarndyce but of pleasing herself. Nothing in her childhood or young adulthood has prepared her to think she has a right to do so. After her first uncontrollably bitter reaction, Esther can only allow herself to use Woodcourt’s proposal as a way of fulfilling her childhood vow: “He had called me the beloved of his life, and had said I would be evermore as dear to him as I was then; and I felt as if my heart would not hold the triumph of having heard those words. My first wild thought had died away. It was not too late to hear them, for it was not too late to be animated by them to be good, true, grateful, and contented” (chap. 61, italics added). Her “selfish” impulse is immediately translated into duty, and she can only think of ways to make herself worthy of Woodcourt’s faith. It is impossible for her to assume that she already is worthy; all her plans are for self-improvement.

The effect of such contradictory impulses can only be paralysis. The need to depend on her guardian and the need to break away from him seem equally strong. Yet a few pages later, the conflict has been mysteriously resolved and Esther is married to Woodcourt. This conclusion to Esther’s history seems to me sheer fantasy. Everything in her narrative has stressed the potent nature of her conflicts and the feebleness of her own will in dealing with them. Indeed, she must not even allow herself to acknowledge them. Such a situation demands a tragic ending—or a deus ex machina. Dickens chooses the latter. Esther’s decision is made for her by Jarndyce, who surrenders her to Woodcourt without even consulting her. The whole scene is dominated by magical and fantastic elements whose function is to dissolve the contradictions inherent in the situation. Where conflict was, there harmony shall be.

All the plans for Esther’s future life deny the presence of conflict or the necessity of exclusive choice. The house where she and Woodcourt will live was purchased and furnished by her guardian. It too is to be called Bleak House, so that Esther will be its mistress after all. It is decorated inside and out like her old home and will even have a replica of the Growlery, where her guardian plans to spend much of his time. Esther had hoped to be vindicated by God, and it is notable that Jarndyce becomes indistinguishable from a benevolent Providence in the book’s concluding section. He denies the part of himself which had once wanted her as a wife: “I am your guardian and your father now” (chap. 64). Esther sees him in a new way: “As I sat looking fixedly at him, and the sun’s rays descended, softly shining through the leaves, upon his bare head, I felt as if the brightness on him must be like the brightness of the Angels” (chap. 64). Such religious references relate to the Ada-Richard plot, which comes to a tragic conclusion simultaneously, and in which it is made clear that only eternity can resolve the conflicts of earthly life: [Cosa que además se relaciona con dos puntos en la trayectoria de Esther: su deseo de resolver su conflicto una vez en el cielo, y la resolución casi extraterrenal de su conflicto] “A smile irradiated his face, as she bent to kiss him. He slowly laid his face down upon her bosom, drew his arms closer round her neck, and with one parting sob began the world. Not this world, O not this! The world that sets this right” (chap. 65).

The treatment of Esther’s internal conflicts has been so detailed, painstaking, and psychologically plausible that the sudden miraculous resolution, in which fantasy elements are presented within a realistic framework, seems totally unconvincing. Yet several critics have treated the change in Esther as a triumphant self-assertion. J. Hillis Miller, for example, writes of “her liberation into an authentic life when she chooses to accept the self she finds herself to be.” I can see no such existential choice. Esther cannot properly be said to choose at all, and much of her old self manages to survive her change of situation. This is illustrated obliquely and symbolically in Ada’s return to Jarndyce after Richard’s death. Esther has treated Ada as a second self throughout the novel, but they are really alternative aspects of a single nature. Esther’s marriage to Woodcourt is possible only after Ada loses her husband and returns to her loving guardian. [Muy siniestro y simbólico]

The two girls change places. Only one of them can venture into the world of love and marriage at a time; the alter ego must stay at home. The psychological complexity of the book is often revealed in such symbolic ways just when the literal events seem least plausible and convincing. In the interstices of Dickens’s magical solution, he cannot help planting these grains of truth.

Dickens characteristically resorts to fantasy whenever his sharp eye for human suffering has uncovered more than he can bear to contemplate. His detailed realistic observation stops short at the borders of despair. The resolution of the Esther plot is only one case in point. There are two other significant instances of a resort to fantasy in the book, the first of which also involves Esther. I have tried to show how psychologically plausible Dickens’s portrait is, but there is one crucial fact this analysis has ignored: that a child brought up in a totally loveless home, as Esther was, is almost surely doomed to grow up unable to love anyone. Yet Esther is an open, affectionate, thoroughly responsive person. [Esto me parece alarmantemente determinista] Here Dickens’s realism comes into conflict with his desire to present his victims as purely victimized. The portrait of the bitter Miss Wade in Little Dorrit suggests that he knew how unlovable a person who had never been loved could be; but Miss Wade is a minor character, not the heroine of the novel, and we are not asked to waste very much sympathy on her. Esther, on the other hand, is an example of Dickens’s myth of the innocent child, whose goodness must be absolute.
